Efficient Integrated Volatility Estimation in the Presence of Infinite Variation Jumps via Debiased Truncated Realized Variations Note: The second author’s research is partially supported by the NSF Grants: DMS-2015323, DMS-1613016. Note: An earlier version of this manuscript was circulated under the title “Efficient Volatility Estimation for Lévy Processes with Jumps of Unbounded Variation". The results therein were constrained to Lévy processes, whereas here we consider a much larger class of Itô semimartingales.
B. Cooper Boniece, José E. Figueroa-López, Yuchen Han

Abstract
Statistical inference for stochastic processes based on high frequency observations has been an active research area for more than two decades. One of the most well-known and widely studied problems has been the estimation of the quadratic variation of the continuous component of an Itô semimartingale with jumps. Several rate- and variance-efficient estimators have been proposed in the literature when the jump component is of bounded variation. However, to date, very few methods can deal with jumps of unbounded variation. By developing new high-order expansions of the truncated moments of a locally stable Lévy process, we propose a new rate- and variance-efficient volatility estimator for a class of Itô semimartingales whose jumps behave locally like those of a stable Lévy process with Blumenthal-Getoor index Y ∈ ( 1 , 8 / 5 ) Y\in(1,8/5) (hence, of unbounded variation). The proposed method is based on a two-step debiasing procedure for the truncated realized quadratic variation of the process and can also cover the case Y < 1 Y<1 . Our Monte Carlo experiments indicate that the method outperforms other efficient alternatives in the literature in the setting covered by our theoretical framework.



1
Introduction

Statistical inference for stochastic processes based on high-frequency observations has attracted considerable attention in the literature for more than two decades. Among the many problems studied to date, arguably none has received more attention than that of the estimation of the continuous (or predictable) quadratic variation of an Itô semimartingale
X=\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
. Specifically, if

\displaystyle X_{t}:=X_{0}+X^{c}_{t}+X^{j}_{t}:=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}dW_{s}+X^{j}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],

(1)

where
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
W=\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
is a Wiener process and
X^{j}=\{X^{j}_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
is a pure-jump Itô semimartingale, our estimation target is

IV_{T}=\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds.

This quantity, also known as the
integrated volatility
or
integrated variance
of
X
, has many applications, especially in finance, where
X
typically models the log-return process of a risky asset and
IV_{T}
measures the overall uncertainty or variability inherent in
X
during the time period
[0,T]
. When
X
is observed at evenly spaced times
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}=T
, in the absence of jumps, an efficient estimator of
IV_{T}
is given by the realized quadratic variation
\widehat{IV}_{T}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}})^{2}
in the so-called high-frequency (or infill) asymptotic regime; i.e., when
\max_{i}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})\to{}0
and
T\equiv t_{n}
is fixed. In the presence of jumps,
\widehat{IV}_{T}
is no longer even consistent for
IV_{T}
, instead converging to
IV_{T}+\sum_{s\leq T}(\Delta X_{s})^{2}
, where
\Delta X_{s}:=X_{s}-X_{s^{-}}
denotes the jump at time
s
. To account for jumps, several estimators have been proposed, among which the most well-known are the truncated realized quadratic variation and the multipower variations. We focus on the first class, which, unlike the second, is both rate- and variance-efficient, in the Cramer-Rao lower bound sense, when jumps are of bounded variation under certain additional conditions.

The truncated realized quadratic variation (TRQV), also called truncated realized volatility, was first introduced by
Mancini 2001
and
Mancini 2004
and is defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta_{i}^{n}X)^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X|\leq\varepsilon\}},

(2)

where
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{n}>0
is a tuning parameter converging to
0
at a suitable rate. Above,
\Delta_{i}^{n}X:=X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}}
is the
i
-th increment of
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
based on evenly spaced observations
X_{t_{0}},\ldots,X_{t_{n}}
over a fixed time interval
[0,T]
(i.e.,
t_{i}=ih_{n}
with
h_{n}=T/n
).
It is shown in
Mancini 2009
that TRQV is consistent when either the jumps have finite activity or stem from an infinite-activity Lévy process.
In a semimartingale model with Lévy jumps of bounded variation,
Cont and Mancini 2011
showed that the TRQV admits a feasible central limit theorem (CLT), provided that
\varepsilon_{n}=ch_{n}^{\omega}
with some

{\omega}\in[\frac{1}{4-Y},\frac{1}{2})
, where
Y\in[0,1)
denotes the Blumenthal-Getoor index.
In
Jacod 2008
, consistency was established for a general Itô semimartingale
X
, and a corresponding CLT is given when the jumps of
X
are of bounded variation. In that case, the TRQV attains the optimal rate and asymptotic variance of
\sqrt{h_{n}}
and
2\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds
, respectively.

However, in the presence of jumps of unbounded variation, arguably the most relevant for financial applications (see, e.g.,
Aït-Sahalia and Jacod 2009
,
Belomestny 2010
,
Figueroa-López 2012
, and the results in Table
1
below), the situation is notably different, and the available literature on TRQV offers an incomplete picture. In
Cont and Mancini 2011
, it is shown that when jumps stem from a Lévy process with stable-like small-jumps of infinite variation, the TRQV estimator
\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)
converges to
IV_{T}
at a rate slower than
\sqrt{h_{n}}
. Further, in
Mancini 2011
it is shown that when the jump component
J
is a symmetric
Y
-stable Lévy process and
\varepsilon_{n}=h_{n}^{\omega}
with
{\omega}\in(0,1/2)
, the decomposition
\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})-IV_{T}=\sqrt{h_{n}}Z_{n}+\mathcal{R}_{n}
holds, where
Z_{n}
converges stably in law to
\mathcal{N}(0,2\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds)
, while
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is precisely of order
\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y}
in the sense that
\mathcal{R}_{n}=O_{P}(\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y})
and
\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y}=O_{P}(\mathcal{R}_{n})
(which decays too slowly to allow for efficiency when
Y>1
). In
Amorino and Gloter 2020
, a smoothed version of the TRQV estimator of the form
\widehat{C}^{Sm}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}})^{2}\varphi((X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}})/\varepsilon)
is considered
1
1

1

The authors in
Amorino and Gloter 2020
, in fact, consider the more general estimation of
\int_{0}^{T}f(X_{s})\sigma_{s}^{2}ds
for functions
f
of polynomial growth, for which
IV_{T}
is a special case.
, where
\varphi\in C^{\infty}
vanishes in
\mathbb{R}\backslash{}(-2,2)
and
\varphi(x)=1
for
x\in(-1,1)
. In that case, using the truncation level
\varepsilon_{n}:=h_{n}^{\omega}
, it is shown that
\widehat{C}^{Sm}_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})-IV_{T}=\sqrt{h_{n}}Z_{n}+\mathcal{R}_{n}
with
\mathcal{R}_{n}
such that
\varepsilon_{n}^{-(2-Y)}\mathcal{R}_{n}\to c_{Y}\int\varphi(u)|u|^{1-Y}du
, for a constant
c_{Y}\neq{}0
, and still
Z_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle st}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,2\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds)
. By taking
\varphi
such that
\int\varphi(u)|u|^{1-Y}du=0
, a “bias-corrected" estimator was considered under the additional condition that
Y<4/3
. Specifically, the resulting estimator is such that, for any
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
,
\widehat{C}^{Sm}_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})-IV_{T}=o_{P}(h_{n}^{1/2-{\tilde{\epsilon}}})
, “nearly" attaining the optimal statistical error
O_{P}(h_{n}^{1/2})
. Unfortunately, the construction of such an estimator requires knowledge or accurate estimation of the jump intensity index
Y
, and no feasible CLT was proved when jumps are of unbounded variation even assuming
Y
is known.

Apart from TQRV-based approaches, efficient estimation of
IV_{T}
when the jumps have unbounded variation is intrinsically limited in the general case. In
Jacod and Reiss 2014
, it was shown that when the jump intensity index
Y>1
, the best possible convergence rate, in a minimax sense, over certain “bounded" classes of semimartingales, is of order
(n\log n)^{-(2-Y)/2}
. Nevertheless, in principle, a faster convergence rate may be attainable if one constrains the process
X
to belong to a certain semiparametric class such as when the jumps exhibit a “locally stable”-like behavior. Obviously, the fastest possible rate one can hope to achieve is
n^{-1/2}
, which coincides with the one attained by the realized quadratic variation in the continuous case and is known to be optimal in a minimax sense.

The first (and to-date, only) rate- and variance-efficient estimator of the integrated volatility known in the literature for semimartingales when
Y>1
was proposed by
Jacod and Todorov 2014
, under a locally-stable assumption on jumps, but with some notable additional restrictions: these results require either that the jump intensity index
Y<3/2,
or that the “small" jumps of the process
X
are ‘‘symmetric"
2
2

2

Jacod and Todorov 2014
also constructed an estimator that is rate-efficient even in the presence of asymmetric jumps, but its asymptotic variance is twice as big as the optimal value
2\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds
and, thus, it is not variance efficient.
. Their estimator is based on locally estimating the volatility from the empirical characteristic function of the process’ increments over disjoint time intervals shrinking to
0
, but still containing an increasing number of observations. It requires two debiasing steps, which are simpler to explain for a Lévy process
X
with symmetric
Y
-stable jump component
X^{j}
. The first debiasing step is meant to reduce the bias introduced when attempting to estimate
\log\mathbb{E}(\cos({u}X_{h_{n}}/\sqrt{h_{n}}))
with
\log\big\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\cos\big({u}\Delta_{i}^{n}X/\sqrt{h_{n}}\big)\big\}
. The second debiasing step is aimed at eliminating the second term
in the expansion
-2\log\mathbb{E}(\cos({u}X_{h_{n}}/\sqrt{h_{n}}))/{u}^{2}=\sigma^{2}+2|\gamma|^{Y}{u}^{Y-2}h_{n}^{1-Y/2}+O(h_{n})
, which otherwise diverges when multiplied by the optimal scaling
h_{n}^{-1/2}=n^{1/2}
. Using an extension of this approach, Jacod and Todorov were able to apply these techniques to a more general class of Itô semimartingales in
Jacod and Todorov 2016
, even allowing any
Y<2
, though only rate-efficient, but not variance-efficient, estimators were ultimately constructed.

On the other hand, in the special case of Lévy processes, efficiency across the full range
0<Y<2
without symmetry requirements has been attained by
Mies 2020
, again under a locally stable assumption, via a generalized method of moments. Specifically, for some suitable smooth functions
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{m}
and a scaling factor
u_{n}\rightarrow\infty
,
Mies 2020
proposed to search for the parameter values
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=(\widehat{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{m})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{j}\big(u_{n}\Delta_{i}^{n}X\big)-\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\Big(f_{j}\big(u_{n}\Delta^{n}_{i}\widetilde{X}\big)\Big)=0,\quad j=1,\ldots,m,

(3)

where
\widetilde{X}
is the superposition of a Brownian motion and independent stable Lévy processes closely approximating
X
in a certain sense. The distribution measure
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
of
\widetilde{X}
depends on some parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{m})
, one of which is the volatility
\sigma
of
X
, and
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\cdot)
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. Aside from the fact that this method can only be applied to Lévy processes, it also suffers from other drawbacks. First, its finite-sample performance critically depends on the chosen moment functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
. Secondly, its implementation is computationally expensive and may lead to numerical issues since it involves solving a system of nonlinear equations (including possible non-existence of solutions to (
3
) over finite samples). Moreover, in addition to the required use of a numerical solver to determine the values of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in (
3
), the expectations appearing therein need to be numerically approximated since explicit expressions for the moments
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(f_{j}(u_{n}\Delta^{n}_{i}\widetilde{X}))
are typically not available. This fact introduces additional numerical errors that complicates its performance.

In this paper, we consider a new method to estimate the integrated volatility
IV_{T}=\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds
of an Itô semimartingale whose jump component is given by a stochastic integral with respect to a tempered-stable-like Lévy process
J
of unbounded variation.
To the best of our knowledge, our method, together with
Jacod and Todorov 2014
, are the only efficient methods to deal with jumps of unbounded variation for semimartingales. The idea is natural. We simply apply debiasing steps similar to those of
Jacod and Todorov 2014
to the TRQV of
Mancini 2009
. To give the heuristics as to why this strategy works, consider a small-time expansion of the truncated moments
\mathbb{E}(X_{h_{n}}^{2k}{\bf 1}_{\{|X_{h_{n}}|\leq{}\varepsilon_{n}\}})
of a Lévy process
X_{t}=bt+\sigma W_{t}+X^{j}_{t}
in the asymptotic regime
h_{n},\varepsilon_{n}\to{}0
with
\varepsilon_{n}/\sqrt{h_{n}}\to\infty
. Using a variety of techniques, including a change of probability measure, Fourier-based methods, and small-large jump decompositions, we show the following two expansions:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X^{2}_{h_{n}}{\bf 1}_{\{|X_{h_{n}}|\leq{}\varepsilon_{n}\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}h_{n}+c_{1}h_{n}\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y}+c_{2}h_{n}^{2}\varepsilon^{-Y}_{n}+{\rm h.o.t.},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{h_{n}}^{2k}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|X_{h_{n}}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\right]

\displaystyle=(2k-1)!!\,\sigma^{2k}h_{n}^{k}+c_{3}\,h_{n}\varepsilon_{n}^{2k-Y}+{\rm h.o.t.},

for certain constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\neq{}0
that are explicitly computed. Hereafter, h.o.t. stands for ‘higher order terms’. The expansions above are the most precise of their type in the literature and are of great interest in their own right. Based on the first expansion above, it is easy to see that the rescaled bias
{\mathbb{E}[h_{n}^{-1/2}(\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\sigma^{2}T)]}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\sigma^{2}T\right)\right]

\displaystyle=Tc_{1}h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y}+Tc_{2}h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{-Y}_{n}+{\rm h.o.t.},

(4)

which suggests the necessity of the condition
h_{n}^{-1/2}\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y}=o(1)
for a feasible CLT for
\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)
at the rate
\sqrt{h_{n}}
. However, together with the (necessary) condition
\varepsilon_{n}/\sqrt{h_{n}}\to\infty
, this can happen only if
Y<1
, and removal of the first terms in (
4
) is consequently necessary for efficient estimation when jumps are of unbounded variation.
To that end, note that for any
\zeta>1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(\zeta^{2-Y}-1)\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y}+{\rm h.o.t.},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta^{2}\varepsilon)-2\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)+\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(\zeta^{2-Y}-1)^{2}\varepsilon_{n}^{2-Y}+{\rm h.o.t.}.

The above formulas motivate the “bias-corrected" estimator

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon;\zeta):=\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\frac{\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)^{2}}{\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta^{2}\varepsilon)-2\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)+\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)},

(5)

which is the essence of the debiasing procedure of
Jacod and Todorov 2014
.
As we shall see, the story is more complicated than what the simple heuristics above suggest.
Our main result shows that, for the class of Itô semimartingales described above, the estimator (
5
) is indeed rate- and variance-efficient provided that
Y\in(1,4/3)
. Furthermore, if
Y\in(1,8/5)
, a second bias-correcting step will achieve both rate- and variance-efficiency (for the case
8/5\leq Y<2
, see remark at the end of Section
3
). Even though our main motivation lies in incorporating jumps of unbounded variation, we show that the debiasing steps will still achieve efficiency in the case that
Y<1
(though, of course, no debiasing is needed in that case because the jumps are of bounded variation).

Though our approach is natural, mathematically establishing its efficiency is highly nontrivial,
starting from the new high-order expansions of the truncated moments of Lévy processes – which, beyond heuristics,
ultimately play a key role in analyzing asymptotics for
the debiasing technique – to the application of Jacod’s stable central limit theorem for semimartingales (in particular, the verification of the asymptotic ‘orthogonality’ condition (2.2.40) in
Jacod and Protter 2011
).
Our Monte Carlo experiments indicate improved performance compared to
Jacod and Todorov 2014
(and also
Mies 2020
) for the important class of CGMY Lévy processes (cf.
Carr et al. 2002
) and for a Heston stochastic volatility model with CGMY jumps in the range of values of
Y
covered by our theoretical framework.

If we limit ourselves to a Lévy model, our approach is more computationally efficient and numerically stable than that in
Mies 2020
3
3

3

Though the method of
Mies 2020
allows for simultaneous estimation of several parameters of the model (such as both
\sigma
and
Y
).
. For more general semimartingales,
our procedure is simpler than that in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
since it does not require an extra debiasing step to correct the nonlinear nature of the logarithmic transformation employed therein nor does it require a symmetrization step to deal with asymmetric jump components. Furthermore, our method does not rely on a ‘localization’ technique
in the sense that it does not need to break the data into disjoint blocks where the integrated volatility is locally estimated. The latter step introduces an additional tuning parameter absent from our method.

Let us emphasize that our method is the first variance- and rate-efficient nonparametric method for integrated volatility free of complete symmetry assumptions on small jumps that is capable of exceeding the limit
Y<3/2
imposed in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
;
Jacod and Todorov 2016
. Symmetry is potentially a strong assumption for financial returns as there is a general belief that significant losses are more likely than significant gains. For instance, recently
Asmussen 2022
examined several empirical studies from the literature and observed that the majority display negative skewness. Of course, skewness may arise from either large or small jumps, though large jump asymmetry has received most attention in empirical work. Indeed, there are few studies to date that estimate the intensity of small positive and negative jumps separately. An exception is
Bianchi 2009
, who, using MLE applied to daily S&P500 index data from 1996-2006, obtained the estimates
\hat{C}_{+}=0.7119
,
\hat{C}_{-}=0.5412
,
\hat{G}=59.94
,
\hat{M}=59.94
,
\hat{Y}_{+}=1.0457
, and
\hat{Y}^{-}=1.1521
under a pure-jump Lévy model with Lévy density
C_{+}e^{-x/G}|x|^{-Y_{+}-1}{\bf 1}_{x>0}+C_{-}e^{-|x|/M}|x|^{-Y_{-}-1}{\bf 1}_{x<0}
, which points to asymmetry in small jumps. For further illustration,
in Table
1
below, we fit a Lévy model
\sigma W_{t}+X_{t}^{j}
, with semi-parametric Lévy density
(C_{+}{\bf 1}_{x>0}+C_{-}{\bf 1}_{x<0})q(x)|x|^{-Y-1}
(here,
q(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x\to{}0}}{{\longrightarrow}}1
). We use Mies’ method of moments (cf.
Mies 2020
) for different stocks and frequencies
4
4

4

As pointed out in
Mies 2020
(see also the paragraph after (
3
) above), numerical issues can arise related to feasibility of the estimating equations associated with the method. We are only presenting the results when the algorithm successfully finishes and yields reasonable values.

over a 1-year period in 2005. It is clear that
\hat{C}_{+}
is different from
\hat{C}_{-}
, sometimes by a relatively large value, indicating further evidence for asymmetry in small jump behavior. Furthermore, all values of
\hat{Y}
are larger than
1
, indicating the presence of a jump component of unbounded variation.

Finally, let us also remark that our result opens the doors to attain rate- and variance-efficient estimators free of symmetry requirements beyond the mark
8/5
or in more general semiparametric models with successive Blumethal-Getoor indices by considering further debiasing steps. These directions will be investigated in further work.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the framework and assumptions as well as some known preliminary results from the literature. Section
3
introduces the debiasing method and main results of the paper. Section
4
illustrates the performance of our method via Monte Carlo simulations and compares it to the method in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
. The proofs of the key results are deferred to appendix sections.

2
Setting and background

In this section, we introduce the model, main assumptions, and some notation.
We consider a 1-dimensional Itô semimartingale
X=(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
of the form (
1
), defined on a complete filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}},\mathbb{P})
. Since it has no impact on the value of the increments of
X
, for simplicity throughout we assume
X_{0}=0
. We assume the jump component
X^{j}
can be decomposed into a sum of an infinite-variation process
X^{j,\infty}
and a finite variation process
X^{j,0}
given, for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, as

\displaystyle X^{j}_{t}

\displaystyle:=X_{t}^{j,\infty}+X_{t}^{j,0}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{s^{-}}dJ_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\int\Big\{\delta_{0}(s,{z})\mathfrak{p}_{0}(ds,dz)+\delta_{1}(s,{z})\mathfrak{p}_{1}(ds,dz)\Big\},

(6)

where

\chi=\{\chi_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
is an adapted process satisfying appropriate integrability conditions,

J:=(J_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is an independent pure-jump Lévy process with Lévy triplet
(b,0,\nu)
,
\mathfrak{p}_{0},\mathfrak{p}_{1},
are Poisson random measures on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
with intensities
\mathfrak{q}_{i}(ds,dz)=ds\otimes\lambda_{i}(dz)
, where the
\lambda_{i}
’s are
\sigma
-finite measures on
\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
is assumed independent of
J
. The specific conditions on
\nu
,
\lambda_{i}
, and on the coefficient processes
\delta_{i}
,
\chi
, and
\sigma
are given below.

The Lévy measure
\nu
is assumed to admit a density
s:\mathbb{R}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
of the form

\displaystyle s(x):=\frac{d\nu}{dx}:=\big(C_{+}{\bf 1}_{(0,\infty)}(x)+C_{-}{\bf 1}_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\big)q(x)\,|x|^{-1-Y}.

(7)

Above,
\mathbb{R}_{0}:=\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
,
C_{\pm}>0
,
Y\in(1,2)
, and
q:\mathbb{R}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a bounded Borel-measurable function satisfying the following conditions:

Assumption 1
.

(i)

q(x)\rightarrow 1
, as
x\rightarrow 0
;

(ii)

there exist
\alpha_{\pm}\neq 0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\big|q(x)-1-\alpha_{+}x\big|x^{-Y-1}dx+\int_{-1}^{0}\big|q(x)-1-\alpha_{-}x\big||x|^{-Y-1}dx<\infty.

These processes are sometimes called “stable-like Lévy processes" and were studied in
Figueroa-López et al. 2016
;
Figueroa-López and
Ólafsson 2016
and many other works. In simple terms, condition (i) above says that the small jumps of the Lévy process
X
behave like those of a
Y
-stable Lévy process with Lévy measure

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}(dx):=\big(C_{+}{\bf 1}_{(0,\infty)}(x)+C_{-}{\bf 1}_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\big)|x|^{-Y-1}dx.

(8)

The condition
Y\in(1,2)
implies that
J
has unbounded variation in that sense that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|J_{t_{i}}-J_{t_{i-1}}|\to{}\infty
, a.s., as the partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}=T
is such that
\max\{t_{i}-t_{i-1}\}\to{}0
.

As discussed in Section
1
, in view of
Jacod and Reiss 2014
, the locally
Y
-stable aspect of Assumption
1
is crucially important for our results, and similar assumptions have been made by other authors (e.g,
Mies 2020
;
Jacod and Todorov 2014
). Though not completely general, the class is still relevant in applications, as many of the models proposed in the literature (especially, in finance) fall within this class. Nevertheless, from a theoretical point of view, it remains to be seen as to what the broadest assumptions may be under which one can still attain estimation efficiency.

As in
Figueroa-López et al. 2016
, it will be important for our analysis to apply a density transformation technique
(
Sato 1999
, Section 6.33)
to “transform" the process
J
into a stable Lévy process. Concretely, we can change the probability measure from
\mathbb{P}
to another locally absolutely continuous measure
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
, under which
W
is still a standard Brownian motion independent of
J
, but, under
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
,
J
has Lévy triplet
(\tilde{b},0,\tilde{\nu})
, where
\widetilde{\nu}(dx)
is given as in (
61
) and
\widetilde{b}:=\bar{b}+\int_{0<|x|\leq 1}x(\tilde{\nu}-\nu)(dx)
. The key assumption above is (ii), which would allow us to decompose the log-density process

U_{t}:=\ln\frac{d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\big|_{\mathscr{F}_{t}}}{d\mathbb{P}\big|_{\mathscr{F}_{t}}},

as a sum of a bounded variation process and two spectrally one-sided
Y
-stable Lévy processes.

Finally, we give the conditions on
\sigma
and the coefficient processes
b
,
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}
, and
\chi
in (
1
) and (
6
).

Assumption 2
.

(i)

\sigma
is càdlàg adapted.

(ii)

The process
\chi
is given as

\chi_{t}=\chi_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b^{\chi}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\Sigma^{\chi}_{s}dB_{s}.

(iii)

The processes
W,B
are Brownian motions independent of
(J,\mathfrak{p}_{0},\mathfrak{p}_{1})
;
(\delta_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{1})
is independent of
(X^{c},J,\chi,\mathfrak{p}_{0},\delta_{0})
;
J
is independent of
\sigma
.

(iv)

The processes
\Sigma^{\chi}
,
b
, and
b^{\chi}
are càdlàg adapted, and
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}
are predictable. There is also a sequence
\{\tau_{n}\}_{n\geq{}1}
of stopping times increasing to infinity, nonnegative
\lambda_{i}(dz)
-integrable functions
H_{i}
, and a positive sequence
\{M_{n}\}_{n\geq{}1}
such that

t\leq\tau_{n}\implies\begin{cases}|\sigma_{t}|+|b_{t}|+|b^{\chi}_{t}|+|\Sigma^{\chi}_{t}|\leq M_{n},\\
|\mathbb{E}(\sigma_{t+s}-\sigma_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t})|+\mathbb{E}(|\sigma_{t+s}-\sigma_{t}|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t})\leq M_{n}s,\\
(|\delta_{0}(t,z)|\wedge 1)^{r_{0}}\leq M_{n}H_{0}(z),\\
(|\delta_{1}(t,z)|\wedge 1)^{r_{1}}\leq M_{n}H_{1}(z),\end{cases}

for some
r_{0}\in[0,\frac{Y}{2+Y})
and
r_{1}\in[0,Y/2)
.

Above, the parameters
r_{0},r_{1}
control the degree of activity in the nuisance finite-variation jump terms. Two such terms are included to allow for a broader range of finite-variation jump activity in our model setup. Note that
Y/2
is always bigger than
Y/(2+Y)
, which shows that when the bounded variation jump component is independent from the other processes, we can incorporate a wider range of jump activity. These restrictions effectively guarantee that the bias introduced by finite variation components are negligible in comparison to leading bias terms arising from the locally-stable jumps in
X
.

3
Main results

In this section, we construct an efficient estimator for the integrated volatility
IV_{T}=\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds
based on the well-studied estimator TRQV (
2
).

Throughout, we assume the process
X=\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
is sampled at
n
evenly spaced observations,
X_{t_{1}},X_{t_{2}},\ldots,X_{t_{n}}
, during a fixed time interval
[0,T]
, where for
i=0,\dots,n
,
t_{i}=t_{i,n}=ih_{n}
with
h_{n}=T/n
, and, for simplicity, assume that
T=1
. As usual, we define the increments of a generic process
V=\{V_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
as
\Delta_{i}^{n}V:=V_{t_{i}}-V_{t_{i-1}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
. We often use the shorthand notation:

V_{i}^{n}=V_{t_{i}},\quad\mathbb{E}_{i}[\cdot]=\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}}].

As mentioned in the introduction, in the presence of jumps of unbounded variation, the TRQV estimator
\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)
is not efficient since it possesses a bias that vanishes at a rate slower than
n^{-1/2}
, the rate at which the “centered” TRQV

\overline{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X\big)^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X|\leq\varepsilon\}}-\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X\big)^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X|\leq\varepsilon\}}\right]\right\},

(9)

admits a CLT.
To overcome this, our idea is to apply the debiasing procedure of
Jacod and Todorov 2014
to the TRQV.
As mentioned in the introduction,
our procedure is simpler than
Jacod and Todorov 2014
since it does not require an extra debiasing step to account for the logarithmic transformation nor does it require a symmetrization step to deal with asymmetric Lévy measures. Furthermore, our method does not have to be applied in each subinterval of a partition of the time horizon, which introduces another tuning parameter.

Before constructing our estimator, we first establish the asymptotic behavior of TRQV (
2
) with a fully specified centering quantity
A(\varepsilon,h)
rather than the inexplicit centering
\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X\big)^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X|\leq\varepsilon\}}\right]

of (
9
). It also characterizes the structure of the bias
A(\varepsilon,h)
in the threshold parameter
\varepsilon
that will ultimately be exploited in our debiasing procedure.
Below and throughout the rest of the paper, we use the usual notation
a_{n}\ll b_{n}
, whenever
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proposition 1
.

Suppose that
Y\in(0,1)\cup(1,8/5)
and
h_{n}^{\frac{3}{2(2+Y)}\wedge\frac{1}{2}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{4-Y}}
. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon):=\sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds-A(\varepsilon,h)\right),

(10)

where

\displaystyle A(\varepsilon,h):=\frac{\bar{C}}{2-Y}\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}ds\varepsilon^{2-Y}-\bar{C}\frac{(Y+1)(Y+2)}{2Y}\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}\sigma^{2}_{s}dsh\varepsilon^{-Y},

(11)

and
\bar{C}:=C_{+}+C_{-}
. Then, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds\right).

(12)

Remark 1
.

As expected, the statement above shows that, when
Y<1
, we have
\sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds\right)
, whenever
h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2(2-Y)}}
and the indices
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
in Assumption
2
are less than
Y
(i.e., the constraints
r_{0}\in[0,\frac{Y}{2+Y})
and
r_{1}\in[0,Y/2)
are needed in Proposition
1
only in the case
Y>1
). Indeed, in this case, since the leading order bias is
O(\varepsilon^{2-Y}),
so taking
\varepsilon\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2(2-Y)}}
renders it asymptotically negligible.

The next theorem establishes the stable convergence (in particular, the convergence rate) of the difference
\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)-\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)
, for some
\zeta>1
, which is the second main technical result we use to deduce the efficiency of our debiased estimator.

Proposition 2
.

Suppose
Y\in(0,1)\cup(1,8/5)
, and
h_{n}^{\frac{4}{8+Y}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{4-Y}}
. With the notation of Proposition
1
, for arbitrary
\zeta>1
,

\displaystyle u_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon_{n})-\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\bar{C}}{4-Y}\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}ds(\zeta^{4-Y}-1)\right),

(13)

as
n\to\infty
, where
u_{n}:=h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon_{n}^{\frac{4-Y}{2}}\to 0
.

Remark 2
.

Note that (
13
) implies that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}^{-Y/2}\left(\frac{\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2-Y}}-\frac{\bar{C}}{2-Y}(\zeta^{2-Y}-1)\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}ds\right)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\bar{C}}{4-Y}(\zeta^{4-Y}-1)\int_{0}^{1}|\gamma_{s}|^{Y}ds\right).

In particular,

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2-Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{}\frac{\bar{C}}{2-Y}(\zeta^{2-Y}-1)\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}ds.

(14)

Expression (
14
) plays a role in our numerical implementation in Section
4
.

We are now in a position to introduce our proposed estimator. To this end, we will exploit the structure of the bias term
A(\varepsilon,h)
in
\varepsilon
. The idea is simple. Suppose that a function
f(x)
takes the form
a+bx^{\alpha}
for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha,b\neq{}0
. Then, it is easy to see that, for any
\zeta>1
,

f(x)-\frac{(f(\zeta x)-f(x))^{2}}{f(\zeta^{2}x)-2f(\zeta x)+f(x)}=a+bx^{\alpha}-\frac{b^{2}x^{2\alpha}(\zeta^{\alpha}-1)^{2}}{bx^{\alpha}(\zeta^{2\alpha}-2\zeta^{\alpha}+1)}=a,

hence, recovering
a
without requiring knowledge of
b
and
\alpha
. These heuristics suggest the following debiasing procedure to successively remove each term appearing in
A(\varepsilon,h)
. For any
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}>1
, in a first step, we compute

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\frac{\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)^{2}}{\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}^{2}\varepsilon)-2\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)+\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)},

(15)

and, in the second step,

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\frac{\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})\right)^{2}}{\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-2\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})+\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})}.

(16)

The next theorem is the main result of the paper. It establishes the rate- and variance-efficiency of the two-step debiased estimator
\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1})
provided
Y\in(0,1)\cup(1,8/5)
.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that
Y\in(0,1)\cup(1,{8/5})
, and
h_{n}^{\frac{4}{8+Y}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{4-Y}\vee\frac{2}{4+Y}}
. Then, for any fixed
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}>1
, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1})-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds\right).

It is customary to use power thresholds of the form
\varepsilon_{n}=c_{0}h_{n}^{\omega}
, where
c_{0}>0
and
{\omega}>0
are some constants
5
5

5

Though, it is shown in
Figueroa-López et al. 2022
that in the case of a Lévy process
X
with jump component
J
as in Section
2
, the MSE-optimal threshold
\varepsilon_{n}^{*}
is such that
\varepsilon_{n}^{*}\sim\sqrt{(2-Y)\sigma^{2}h_{n}\ln(1/h_{n})}
, as
n\to\infty
.
. In that case, the assumption
h_{n}^{\frac{4}{8+Y}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{4-Y}\vee{\frac{2}{4+Y}}}
in Theorem
1
becomes

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{4-Y}\vee{\frac{2}{4+Y}}\right)<\omega<\frac{4}{8+Y}.

(17)

Remark 3
.

Note that, if
Y>1
– the case where debiasing is strictly necessary for estimation efficiency – the value of
\omega=5/12
satisfies the above constraint (
17
) for any value
Y
of the possible range
4/5<Y<8/5
(on relaxations of these constraints, see Remark
5
).

Remark 4
.

The case
Y=1
is excluded from the above statements since part of our arguments rely on moment estimates for the truncated increments of
Y
-stable Lévy processes, whose characteristic function differs slightly when
Y=1
, though this case can be handled similarly with minor adjustments to our arguments.

Remark 5
.

As a consequence of the proof of Theorem
1
, it follows that if
1<Y<4/3
, then only one debiasing step is needed to achieve efficiency. That is, for
1<Y<4/3
, we already have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds\right),

whenever
h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2Y}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{2}{4+Y}}
.
If
4/3\leq Y<8/5
, the proof of Theorem
1
shows a second debiasing step is required. These two facts suggest that further debiasing steps similar to (
15
)-(
16
) could be used to handle values of
Y
larger than
8/5
, or more broadly, less restrictive conditions on the jump measure of X at zero. This conjecture requires significant further analysis beyond the scope of the present paper and, hence, we leave it for future research.

4
Monte Carlo performance with CGMY jumps

In this section, we study the performance of the two-step debiasing procedure of the previous section in two settings: the case of a Lévy process with a CGMY jump component
J
(cf.
Carr et al. 2002
) and a Heston stochastic volatility model with again a CGMY jump component. Following
Jacod and Todorov 2014
, we also consider variants of our debiasing procedure that make use of the sign of the bias terms that lead to further improved finite-sample performance.

4.1
Constant volatility

We start by considering simulated data from the model (
1
) and (
6
),
where the coefficient processes
\sigma
,
b
, and
\chi
are constants,
\delta_{0}=\delta_{1}\equiv 0
(no bounded variation components), and
\{J_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
is a CGMY process, independent of the Brownian motion
\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq{}0}
, with Lévy measure having a
q
-function, in the notation of (
7
), of the form:

\displaystyle q(x)=e^{-Mx}{\bf 1}_{(0,\infty)}(x)+e^{Gx}{\bf 1}_{(-\infty,0)}(x),

and
C_{+}=C_{-}=C
. Thus, the conditions of Assumption
1
are satisfied with
\alpha_{+}=-M
and
\alpha_{-}=G
. For simplicity we take
b=0
and
\chi=1
, and adopt the parameter setting

\displaystyle C=0.028,\quad G=2.318,\quad M=4.025,

(18)

which are similar to those used in
Figueroa-López and
Ólafsson 2016
6
6

6

Figueroa-López and
Ólafsson 2016
considers the asymmetric case
\nu(dx)=C_{{\rm sgn}(x)}\bar{q}(x)|x|^{-1-Y}\,dx
with
C_{+}=0.015
and
C_{-}=0.041
. Here, we take
C=(C_{+}+C_{-}))/2
in order to simplify the simulation of the model. The parameter values of
C_{+}
,
C_{-}
,
G
, and
M
used in
Figueroa-López and
Ólafsson 2016
were taken from
Kawai 2010
, who calibrated the tempered stable model using market option prices.
, and take
\sigma=0.2
,
0.4
, and
Y=1.25
,
1.35
,
1.5
,
1.7
, respectively. We fix
T=1
year and

n=252(6.5)(60)
, which corresponds to a frequency of
1
minute (assuming
252
trading days and a
6.5
-hour trading period each day).

In a fashion similar to
Jacod and Todorov 2014
, for the threshold
\varepsilon=c_{0}h^{\omega}
, we take
c_{0}=\sigma_{BV}
, where

\sigma_{BV}^{2}:=\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{i=2}^{n}|\Delta_{i-1}^{n}X||\Delta_{i}^{n}X|,

which is the standard bipower variation estimator of
\sigma^{2}
first introduced by
Barndorff-Nielsen 2004
. For the value of
{\omega}
we take
{\omega}=\frac{5}{12}
, which, as mentioned above, satisfies the condition (
17
) for any
Y\in(1,8/5)
.
We compare the performance of the following estimators:

1.

TRQV:
\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X\big)^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X|\leq\varepsilon\}}
;

2.

1-step debiasing estimator removing positive bias:

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},p_{1})=\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\eta_{1}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right),\\
\text{where}\quad\eta_{1}=\frac{\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\varepsilon)}{\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\zeta_{1}^{2}\varepsilon)-2\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)+\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\varepsilon)}\vee 0,\end{gathered}

(19)

with
\zeta_{1}=1.45
and
p_{1}=0.6
, which were selected to achieve favorable performance across all considered values of
Y
and
\sigma
. If
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},p_{1})
is negative, we recompute
\eta_{1}
with
\varepsilon=2\varepsilon/3
. This method is inspired by
Jacod and Todorov 2014
and is motivated by the following decomposition of the bias correction term of (
15
) into a product of two factors:

\frac{\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)}{\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}^{2}\varepsilon)-2\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)+\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)}\times\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right),

where, due to (
14
), the first factor estimates
(\zeta_{1}^{2-Y}-1)^{-1}
, which is positive. So, we should expect
\eta_{1}>0
.

3.

With
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon):=\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},p_{1})
defined as in Step 1, the 2-step debiasing estimator removing negative bias is given by:

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1},p_{2},p_{1})=\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)-\eta_{2}\left(\left(\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)-\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\right)\vee 0\right),\\
\text{where}\quad\eta_{2}=\frac{\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\zeta_{2}\varepsilon,)-\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\varepsilon)}{\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\zeta_{2}^{2}\varepsilon)-2\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)+\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\varepsilon)}\wedge 0,\end{gathered}

(20)

with
\zeta_{1}=1.2
,
\zeta_{2}=1.2
,
p_{1}=0.65
, and
p_{2}=0.75
.
If it turns out that
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1},p_{2},p_{1})
is negative, we recompute
\eta_{2}
with
\varepsilon=2\varepsilon/3
. The reason for this adjustment is the fact that
\eta_{2}
is expected to be negative since it serves as estimate of
(\zeta_{2}^{-Y}-1)^{-1}
. The values of the tuning parameters
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},p_{1},p_{2}
were selected for ‘overall favorable’ performance for all considered values of
Y
and
\sigma
.

We further compare the simulated performance of the above estimators to the estimators proposed in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
and
Mies 2020
. Specifically, we use the equation (5.3) in the paper
Jacod and Todorov 2014
:

\begin{gathered}\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}(u_{n},\zeta)=\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(u_{n})-\eta\left(\left(\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(\zeta u_{n})-\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(u_{n})\right)\wedge 0\right),\\
\text{with}\quad\eta=\frac{\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(p_{0}\,\zeta u_{n})-\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(p_{0}\,u_{n})}{\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(p_{0}\,\zeta^{2}u_{n})-2\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(p_{0}\,\zeta u_{n})+\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}(p_{0}\,u_{n})}\wedge 0,\end{gathered}

where
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT}}
denotes their “nonsymmetrized” two-step debiased estimator (see Eq. (3.1) in therein). For the tuning parameters, we take

\zeta=1.5,\quad u_{n}=(\ln(1/h_{n}))^{-1/30}/\sigma_{BV},\quad p_{0}=0.2,

where the values of
\zeta
and
u_{n}
were those suggested by
Jacod and Todorov 2014
and the value of
p_{0}
was chosen for favorable estimation performance.
Note that, since a Lévy model has constant volatility, it is not necessary to localize the estimator and, hence, we treat the 1-year data as one block, which corresponds to taking
k_{n}=252(6.5)(60)
in the notation of
Jacod and Todorov 2014
.
For the moment estimator proposed in
Mies 2020
, we use the same moment functions and the parameter settings as suggested by
Mies 2020
. We denote this estimator
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},4}
. We also examine the performance of another moment estimator, denoted
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
, that is computed under a similar algorithm to
Mies 2020
but with
3
different moment functions suggested in
Figueroa-López et al. 2022
.
We remark that that the moment functions used in the construction of
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
do not satisfy the strict constraints imposed in
Mies 2020
, and therefore the asymptotic efficiency of the estimator
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
has not been established. We refer to
Figueroa-López et al. 2022
for more details about the computations of
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
and
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},4}
.

The sample means, standard deviations (SDs), the average and SD of relative errors, the mean squared errors (MSEs), and median of absolute deviations (MADs) for each of the estimators described above are reported in Tables
3
-
11
. In addition, we show the results corresponding to
‘case-by-case favorably-tuned’ versions of
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
; i.e., their tuning parameters were chosen for achieving the ‘best’ performance for each pair
(Y,\sigma)
based on a grid search; we distinguish these estimators and their corresponding tuning parameters by the superscript
∗
. That is,
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime\prime}
is based on the choices
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},p_{1},p_{2})=(1.2,1.2,.65,.75)
across all considered values of
Y
and
\sigma
. With these values of the tuning parameters,
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime\prime}
exhibits generally good performance overall. However, for each given fixed pair
(Y,\sigma)
, its counterpart
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime\prime*}
is tuned to have superior performance for those particular values of
Y
and
\sigma
. For instance, as shown in Table
3
, when
Y=1.25
and
\sigma=0.2
, the estimator
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime\prime}
attains an MSE of
1.45\times 10^{-7}
, whereas the choice of parameters
(\zeta_{1}^{*},\zeta_{2}^{*},p_{1}^{*},p_{2}^{*})=(1.35,1.20,0.5,0.85)
leads to an MSE of

4.70\times 10^{-8}
for
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime\prime*}
.

We provide a broad summary of our simulation results. For the estimators using the case-by-case tuned parameters
(\zeta_{1}^{*},\zeta_{2}^{*},p_{1}^{*},p_{2}^{*})
, based on both MSE and MAD,
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime*}
outperforms every other estimator considered in each setting, except when
Y=0.8
when the performance between
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime*}
and
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}^{*}
is comparable (c.f. Figure
1
, top row) but
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}^{*}
has a slight edge in both MAD and MSE when
\sigma=0.4
and a slight edge in MAD when
\sigma=0.2
. Using the parameters
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},p_{1},p_{2})=(1.2,1.2,.65,.75)
, compared to the method of
Mies 2020
,
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
outperforms
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},4}
in all cases, as measured by MSE and MAD in Tables
3
-
11
.
When
\sigma=0.2
and
Y=1.25
, or when
\sigma=0.4
and
Y=1.25,1.35,1.5,1.7
,
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
outperforms
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
(though, as noted earlier, computation of
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
is much faster and more numerically stable than that of either
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
or
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},4}
).
Next, compared with
Jacod and Todorov 2014
, when
\sigma=0.2
and
Y=1.25,1.35,1.5
, or when
\sigma=0.4
and
Y=1.25
or
1.35
,
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
has superior performance compared to
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
as measured by MSE and MAD. Generally,
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
significantly outperforms
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}
. Table
5
also shows that, though when
\sigma=0.2
and
Y=1.5
,
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
has larger MSE and MAD than
\widehat{C}_{n}
and
\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}
, it still performs better than
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
: the MSE and MAD of
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
in these cases are approximately 22% and 85% of those of
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
, respectively.
Tables
6
and
11
show that, when
Y=1.7
, which is not covered by our theoretical framework (see Remark
5
),
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
has slightly larger MSE and MAD than
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
. Overall, we conclude that our debiasing procedure outperforms
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
when
Y=1.25,1.35,1.5
, and outperforms
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},4}
in all parameter settings considered, and the estimation performance of
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
and
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
is comparable when
Y=0.8
.

We also study the asymptotic approximation for the sampling distribution of
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
and
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}
based on the case-by-case optimally-tuned estimators
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime*}
and
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}^{*}
.
Figure
1
shows their normalized simulated sampling distributions,
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}-\sigma^{2})/\sqrt{2\sigma^{4}}
, and the theoretical asymptotic normal distribution,
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Compared to
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}^{*}
, the simulated distribution of
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime*}
yields a better match with the asymptotic normal distribution, especially for
Y\leq 1.5
. Note that, in general, the values of
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime*}
are much less spread out than those of
\widehat{C}_{\text{JT},53}^{*}
. Though the simulated distributions of
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
and
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},4}
are not shown in Figure
1
, we can see that
\widetilde{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime*}
performs much better than
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},3}
and
\widehat{C}_{\text{M},4}
as seen in Tables
3
-
11
.

4.2
Stochastic volatility

In this section, we apply our two-step debiasing procedure to estimate the daily integrated variance under a stochastic volatility model with a CGMY jump component and compare it with the estimator of Jacod and Todorov
Jacod and Todorov 2014
.

Specifically, we consider the following Heston model:

\displaystyle X_{t}=1+\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{V_{s}}\,dW_{s}+J_{t},\quad V_{t}=\theta+\int_{0}^{t}\kappa\big(\theta-V_{s}\big)\,ds+\xi\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{V_{s}}\,dB_{s},

where
\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
\{B_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
are two correlated standard Brownian motions with correlation
\rho
and
\{J_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a CGMY Lévy process independent of
\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
\{B_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. The parameters are set as

\displaystyle\kappa=5,\quad\xi=0.5,\quad\theta=0.16,\quad{\rho=-0.5}.

The values of
\kappa
,
\xi
, and
\rho
above are borrowed from
Zhang et al. 2005
. The CGMY parameters are the same as those in the previous section.

We consider
1
-min observations over a one-year (
252
days) time horizon with
6.5
trading hours per day. We break each path into
252
blocks (one for each day) and estimate the integrated volatility
IV=\int_{t}^{t+1/252}V_{s}ds
for each day (
t=0,1/252,\dots,251/252
). As suggested and used in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
,
to improve the stability of the estimates, the estimated bias terms in (
19
) and (
20
) are split into two components each:
\big(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\big)
and
\big(\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},p_{1})-\widetilde{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},p_{1})\big)
. These are computed
using the data in each day, and the factors
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
, which only depend on
Y
, are computed using the data during the whole time horizon. In practice, one would precompute
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
using historical data over 1 year and use those values to compute the daily integrated volatility afterward. The precise formulas for our estimators are described below:

1.

1-step debiasing estimator removing positive bias:

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})_{t}=\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)_{t}-\eta_{1}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)_{t}-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)_{t}\right),

\displaystyle\eta_{1}=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{251}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}-\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}\right)}{\sum_{i=0}^{251}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\zeta_{1}^{2}\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}-2\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}+\widehat{C}_{n}(p_{1}\,\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}\right)}\vee 0,

2.

With the estimator
\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)_{t}:=\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})_{t}
defined in Step 1 above, the 2-step debiasing estimator removing negative bias is given by:

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1})_{t}=\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)_{t}-\eta_{2}\left(\left(\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)_{t}-\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)_{t}\right)\vee 0\right),

\displaystyle\eta_{2}=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{251}\left(\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}-\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}\right)}{\sum_{i=0}^{251}\left(\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\zeta_{2}^{2}\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}-2\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}+\overline{C}_{n,pb}^{\prime}(p_{2}\,\varepsilon)_{\frac{i}{252}}\right)}\wedge 0,

with the same parameters used in the previous subsection
\zeta_{1}=1.2
,
\zeta_{2}=1.2
,
p_{1}=0.65
, and
p_{2}=0.75
.

For the estimator of
Jacod and Todorov 2014
, we use equation (5.3) therein with tuning parameter
k_{n}=130
(number of observation in each block),
\xi=1.5
, and
u_{n}=(-\ln h_{n})^{-1/30}/\sqrt{BV}
(these values were suggested in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
). Here,
BV
is the bipower variation of the previous day. The resulting estimator is denoted as
\overline{C}_{\text{JT},53}
. To assess the accuracy of the different methods, we compute the Median Absolute Deviation (MAD) around the true value,
IV_{t}=\int_{t}^{t+1/252}V_{s}ds
, and the MSE, i.e. the sample mean of
(\widehat{IV}_{t}-IV_{t})^{2}
, for 5 arbitrarily chosen days over
1000
simulation paths.

The results are shown in Table
12
. The last column of Table
12
shows the sample means of the MSE and MAD over the 252 days for the different estimators. When
Y=0.8,1.25,1.35,1.5
, all MSEs and MADs of
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
are smaller than those of
\overline{C}_{\text{JT},53}
, with the MSE of
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
typically half of that of
\overline{C}_{\text{JT},53}
or smaller. For the case
Y=1.7
(outside the scope of our theoretical framework), the MSE and MAD of
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
is slightly larger than those of
\overline{C}_{\text{JT},53}
.

This behavior can also be observed in Figure
2
, which shows the true daily integrated volatility (dashed red line) for one fixed simulated path compared with the estimates corresponding to
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
(solid black line) and
\overline{C}_{\text{JT},53}
in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
(dotted blue line). From the figure, we conclude that for this specific stochastic volatility model, our debiasing method achieves significant improvement when
Y\leq{}1.5
. For
Y=1.7
, both estimators
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
and
\overline{C}_{\text{JT},53}
are very close and significantly overestimate the true daily integrated volatility for this simulated path. This changes from path to path, though
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
and
\overline{C}_{\text{JT},53}
are typically close when
Y=1.7
.

Figure 2:
Plots of daily integrated volatility estimates
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1},p_{2},p_{1})_{(\frac{t}{252},\frac{t+1}{252}]}
. Except for a change in the jump index
Y
of the CGMY component, the same path of
X_{t}
is for each plot. Above are the plots for
Y=0.8,1.25,1.5,1.7
(
Y=1.35
is similar to
Y=1.25
and was removed to save space). The dashed red line corresponds to the true daily integrated volatility, while the solid black (respectively, blue) line corresponds to the daily estimates using our debiasing estimator
\overline{C}_{n,nb}^{\prime\prime}
(respectively, the estimator given in expression (5.3) in
Jacod and Todorov 2014
).

Appendix A
Proofs of the main results

Throughout all appendices, we routinely make use of the following fact: under the assumption
h_{n}^{4/(8+Y)}\ll\varepsilon_{n}
made throughout Section
3
, it holds
\exp\big(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}_{n}}{2\sigma^{2}h_{n}}\big)\ll h_{n}^{s}
for any
s>0
. For notational simplicity, we also often omit the subscript
n
in
h_{n}
and
\varepsilon_{n}
. We denote by
C
or
K
generic constants independent of
n
that may be different from line to line. In all proofs, based on Assumption
2
, by a standard localization argument, we may assume without restriction that
|\sigma|,|b|,|b^{\chi}|,|\chi|,|\Sigma^{\chi}|
, are almost surely bounded by a nonrandom constant, and
(|\delta_{i}(t,z)|\wedge 1)^{r_{i}}\leq KH_{i}(z)
,
i=0,1
. Further, we have the following estimates valid for all
s,t>0
:

\mathbb{E}\left(\left.|\chi_{t+s}-\chi_{t}|^{p}\right|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)\leq{}Ks^{p/2},\quad p\geq 1;\quad\mathbb{E}\left(\left.|\sigma_{t+s}-\sigma_{t}|^{2}\right|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)\leq{}Ks.

In the proofs below, we will show that we can neglect the finite variation jump component
X^{j,0}
and prove the results for the Itô semimartingale

X^{\prime}:=X-X^{j,0}=X^{c}_{t}+X_{t}^{j,\infty};

i.e., the process consisting of the continuous component
X^{c}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}b_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}dW_{s}
and the infinite variation jump component
X_{t}^{j,\infty}=\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{s^{-}}dJ_{s}
.

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Note that
\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)
in (
10
)
can be decomposed as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X|\leq\varepsilon\}}-\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{n}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}-\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\big]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{n}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\big]-\sigma_{t_{i-1}}^{2}h-\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)h\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sigma_{t_{i-1}}^{2}h+\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)h\right)-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds-A(\varepsilon,h)\right)

\displaystyle=:T_{0}+T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3},

where
\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)
is defined as in (
32
). Lemma
5
implies
T_{0}=o_{P}(1)
, and Lemma
2
directly implies that
T_{2}=o_{P}(1)
.
We also have
T_{3}=o_{P}(1)
, which follows from the fact that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}_{t_{i-1}}h-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds=o_{P}(n^{-1/2}),

\varepsilon^{2-Y}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\chi_{t_{i-1}}|^{Y}h-\varepsilon^{2-Y}\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}ds=o_{P}(n^{-1/2}),

\text{and}\quad h\varepsilon^{-Y}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\chi_{t_{i-1}}|^{Y}\sigma_{t_{i-1}}^{2}h-h\varepsilon^{-Y}\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds=o_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

The above is established in Lemma
10
of
D
.

We now show
T_{1}\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\big(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds\big)
by applying the martingale difference CLT (Theorem 2.2.15 of
Jacod and Protter 2011
), which will complete the proof. Define

\xi_{i}^{n}:=\sqrt{n}\left(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}-\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\big)\right).

We first need to show that

V_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[(\xi_{i}^{n})^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds
.
The left-hand side can be written as

\displaystyle V_{n}=n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{4}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\big]-\big[\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\big]^{2}\right).

Lemmas
2
and
4
imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime})^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq{}\varepsilon\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t_{i-1}}^{2}h+O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{2-Y}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[\left(\Delta_{i}^{n}{X^{\prime}}\right)^{4}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}{X^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\right]

\displaystyle=3\sigma^{4}_{t_{i-1}}h^{2}+O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{4-Y}).

Since, due to our conditions on
\varepsilon
,
h\varepsilon^{4-Y}\ll h^{2}
, we clearly have

\displaystyle V_{n}=n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(3\sigma^{4}_{t_{i-1}}h^{2}+o_{P}(h^{2})-\big(\sigma_{t_{i-1}}^{2}h+O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{2-Y})\big)^{2}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds.

Next, we show that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[(\xi_{i}^{n})^{4}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i-1}

\displaystyle\left[(\xi_{i}^{n})^{4}\right]

\displaystyle\leq n^{2}K\Big(\mathbb{E}_{i-1}[(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{8}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}]+\big[\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\big]^{4}\Big).

Lemma
4
implies that,

\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[\left(\Delta_{i}^{n}{X^{\prime}}\right)^{2k}\,{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}{X^{\prime}}|\leq\varepsilon\}}\right]=O_{P}(h^{k})+O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{2k-Y}),

for
k\geq{}1
. Then,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[(\xi_{i}^{n})^{4}\right]=n^{3}\big(O_{P}(h^{4})+O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{8-Y})\big)=o_{P}(1)
, since our assumption
\varepsilon\ll h^{\frac{1}{4-Y}}
implies that
\varepsilon\ll h^{\frac{1}{4-Y/2}}
, which is equivalent to
n^{3}h\varepsilon^{8-Y}\ll 1
. It remains to check the condition (2.2.40) in
Jacod and Protter 2011
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[\xi_{i}^{n}({\Delta_{i}^{n}M})\right]\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,0,

(21)

when
M=W
or
M
is a square-integrable martingale orthogonal to
W
. This is proved in Lemma
11
of
D
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2
.

We consider the decomposition:

\displaystyle u_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon_{n})-\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{n}^{\frac{Y-4}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}-\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon_{n}^{\frac{Y-4}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}-\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}\big]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon_{n}^{\frac{Y-4}{2}}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}\big]-\widehat{A}_{i}(\zeta\varepsilon,h)h+\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)h\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon_{n}^{\frac{Y-4}{2}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{A}_{i}(\zeta\varepsilon,h)-\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)\right)h-A(\zeta\varepsilon,h)+A(\varepsilon,h)\right)

\displaystyle=:T_{0}+T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3},

where
\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)
is defined as in (
32
).
From Lemma
5
we have

T_{0}=n\varepsilon^{(Y-4)/2}o_{P}(h\varepsilon^{2-Y/2})=o_{P}(1).

Lemma
3
shows that
T_{2}=o_{P}(1)
.
To show that
T_{3}=o_{P}(1)
, Lemma
10
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{A}_{i}(\zeta\varepsilon,h)-\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)\right)h-A(\zeta\varepsilon,h)+A(\varepsilon,h)=o_{P}(h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{2-Y})+o_{P}(h^{\frac{3}{2}}\varepsilon^{-Y}),

and each of the terms above is
o_{P}(\varepsilon^{\frac{4-Y}{2}})
since
h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{2-Y}\gg h^{\frac{3}{2}}\varepsilon^{-Y}
and
h^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{2-Y}\ll\varepsilon^{\frac{4-Y}{2}}
, which is implied from our assumption
h^{\frac{4}{8+Y}}\ll\varepsilon
since
\frac{1}{Y}>\frac{4}{8+Y}
.
It remains to show
T_{1}\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\big(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{4}ds\big)
, for which we shall again use Theorem 2.2.15 in
Jacod and Protter 2011
. Define

\tilde{\xi}_{i}^{n}:=\varepsilon^{\frac{Y-4}{2}}\left(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}-\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big(\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}\big)\right).

We first need to compute
V_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[(\tilde{\xi}_{i}^{n})^{2}\right]
. We clearly have

\displaystyle V_{n}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{Y-4}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{4}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}\big]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\left.-\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big[\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}\big]^{2}\right).

Lemmas
2
and
4
imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i-1}(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime})^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq{}\zeta\varepsilon\}}

\displaystyle=O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{2-Y}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left(\Delta_{i}^{n}{X^{\prime}}\right)^{4}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}{X^{\prime}}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{C}|\chi_{t_{i-1}}|^{Y}}{4-Y}\,h\varepsilon^{4-Y}(\zeta^{4-Y}-1)+o_{P}(h\varepsilon^{4-Y}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle V_{n}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{Y-4}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\bar{C}}{4-Y}|\chi_{t_{i-1}}|^{Y}\,h\varepsilon^{4-Y}(\zeta^{4-Y}-1)+o_{P}(h\varepsilon^{4-Y})+O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{2-Y})^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{\bar{C}}{4-Y}(\zeta^{4-Y}-1)\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}ds,

since
\varepsilon^{Y-4}n(h\varepsilon^{2-Y})^{2}=h\varepsilon^{-Y}\ll 1
.
Next, we show

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}(\tilde{\xi}_{i}^{n})^{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i-1}(\tilde{\xi}_{i}^{n})^{4}\leq K\varepsilon^{2Y-8}\Big(\mathbb{E}_{i-1}[

\displaystyle(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{8}{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}]

\displaystyle+\big[\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\big(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}\big)^{2}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}\big]^{4}\Big).

Lemma
4
implies that for
k\geq{}1
,

\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left(\Delta_{i}^{n}{X^{\prime}}\right)^{2k}\,{\bf 1}_{\{\varepsilon<|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq\zeta\varepsilon\}}=O_{P}(h\varepsilon^{2k-Y}),

and thus,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}(\tilde{\xi}_{i}^{n})^{4}=\varepsilon^{2Y-8}O_{P}(\varepsilon^{8-Y})=o_{P}(1)
.
It remains to check the condition (2.2.40) in
Jacod and Protter 2011
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[\tilde{\xi}_{i}^{n}({\Delta_{i}^{n}M})\right]\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,0,

(22)

when
M=W
or
M
is a square-integrable martingale orthogonal to
W
. The proof is much more involved and technical than that of (
111
) and is given in Lemma
12
of
D
.∎

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Recall the notation of Propositions
1
and
2
.
In addition, we set up the following notation:

\displaystyle a_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\bar{C}}{2-Y}\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}ds\varepsilon^{2-Y}=:{\kappa_{1}}\varepsilon^{2-Y},

\displaystyle a_{2}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=a_{2}(\varepsilon,h):=-\bar{C}\frac{(Y+1)(Y+2)}{2Y}\int_{0}^{1}|\chi_{s}|^{Y}\sigma^{2}_{s}dsh\varepsilon^{-Y}=:{\kappa_{2}}h\varepsilon^{-Y},

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}

\displaystyle:=u_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)=O_{P}(1),

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}

\displaystyle:={u_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\zeta^{2}_{1}\varepsilon)-2\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)+\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)}=O_{P}(1),

where the stochastic boundedness of
\Phi_{n}
and
\Psi_{n}
is a consequence of Proposition
2
.
The proof is obtained in two steps.

Step 1.

We first analyze the behavior of
\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})={\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)}-\widehat{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)
, where

\displaystyle\widehat{a}_{1}(\varepsilon):=\frac{\left(\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)^{2}}{\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}^{2}\varepsilon)-2\widehat{C}_{n}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)+\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)}.

(23)

If we let
\eta_{1}(\zeta)=\zeta^{2-Y}-1
and
\eta_{2}(\zeta)=\zeta^{-Y}-1
, then, for
i=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{i}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon)-a_{i}(\varepsilon)=\eta_{i}(\zeta_{1})a_{i}(\varepsilon),\quad a_{i}(\zeta_{1}^{2}\varepsilon)-2a_{i}(\zeta_{1}\varepsilon))+a_{i}(\varepsilon)=\eta_{i}^{2}(\zeta_{1})a_{i}(\varepsilon).\end{split}

For simplicity, we often omit the variable
\zeta_{1}
on
\eta_{i}(\zeta_{1})
. Also, note that, by definition,
\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\sqrt{h}\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)+\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds+A(h,\varepsilon)
and
A(\varepsilon,h)=a_{1}(\varepsilon)+a_{2}(\varepsilon,h)
. Therefore, we may write

\displaystyle{\widehat{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\eta_{1}a_{1}(\varepsilon)+\eta_{2}a_{2}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}u_{n}\Phi_{n})^{2}}{\eta_{1}^{2}a_{1}(\varepsilon)+{\eta_{2}^{2}a_{2}(\varepsilon)}+\sqrt{h}u_{n}\Psi_{n}}.

(24)

By expanding the squares in the numerator and using the notation

\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon):={a_{1}(\varepsilon)+\widetilde{\eta}_{2}a_{2}(\varepsilon):=a_{1}(\varepsilon)+\frac{2\eta_{1}\eta_{2}-\eta_{2}^{2}}{\eta_{1}^{2}}a_{2}(\varepsilon)},

we may express
\widehat{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)
as

\displaystyle\widehat{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)+\frac{{\eta_{2}^{2}(1-\widetilde{\eta}_{2})}a^{2}_{2}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}\left[2u_{n}\Phi_{n}(\eta_{1}a_{1}(\varepsilon)+\eta_{2}a_{2}(\varepsilon))-\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)u_{n}\Psi_{n}\right]+hu_{n}^{2}\Phi_{n}^{2}}{\eta_{1}^{2}a_{1}(\varepsilon)+{\eta_{2}^{2}a_{2}(\varepsilon)}+\sqrt{h}u_{n}\Psi_{n}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}\times\frac{O(h^{3/2}\varepsilon^{-2Y})+O_{P}(u_{n}\varepsilon^{2-Y})+O_{P}(\sqrt{h}u_{n}^{2})}{\eta_{1}^{2}a_{1}(\varepsilon)+{o(a_{1}(\varepsilon))+O_{P}(\sqrt{h}u_{n})}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}\times\frac{O(h^{3/2}\varepsilon^{-2-Y})+O_{P}(u_{n})+O_{P}(h^{-1/2}\varepsilon^{2})}{\eta_{1}^{2}\kappa_{1}+{o(1)}+O_{P}(\varepsilon^{Y/2})}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)+{\sqrt{h}\times O_{P}(u_{n}),}

(25)

where in the last equality we use our assumption
h_{n}^{\frac{4}{8+Y}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}
to conclude that
h^{3/2}\varepsilon^{-2-Y}\ll u_{n}
.
Then, we see that
\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})=\widehat{C}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\widehat{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)
is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h}\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)+\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds+A(h,\varepsilon)-\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)+O_{P}(h^{1/2}u_{n})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h}\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds+a_{1}(\varepsilon)+a_{2}(\varepsilon,h)-[a_{1}(\varepsilon)+\widetilde{\eta}_{2}a_{2}(\varepsilon)]+O_{P}(h^{1/2}u_{n})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h}\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)+\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds+a^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon)+O_{P}(h^{1/2}u_{n}),

(26)

where
a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=(1-\widetilde{\eta}_{2})a_{2}(\varepsilon)
.
So,

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{n}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds-a^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon)\right)=\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)+O_{P}(u_{n}),

(27)

where the
O_{P}(u_{n})
term is a consequence of expression (
26
). Then, by Proposition
2
,

\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)+O_{P}(u_{n})\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\Big(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{4}_{s}ds\Big),

(28)

since
u_{n}\to 0
by our Assumption
\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{4-Y}}
. Note that if
\varepsilon_{n}\gg h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2Y}}
, then
\sqrt{n}a_{2}^{\prime}\ll 1
and in place of (
25
) we have
\widetilde{a}_{1}(\varepsilon)+{\sqrt{h}\times o_{P}(1)}
, from which we conclude that

\sqrt{n}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\Big(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{4}_{s}ds\Big).

Step 2.
Now we analyze
{\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1})}=\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
, where

\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}):=\frac{\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})\right)^{2}}{\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-2\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})+\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})}.

For simplicity, we omit the dependence on
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}
in
\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1}),C_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
, etc.
First, analogous to
\Phi_{n},\Psi_{n}
defined in Step 1, we define

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle u_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{n}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)-\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\right)=u_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\zeta\varepsilon)-\widetilde{Z}_{n}(\varepsilon)+O_{P}(u_{n})\right)=O_{P}(1),

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}_{n}:=

\displaystyle u_{n}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{n}(\zeta^{2}_{2}\varepsilon)-2\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{n}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)+\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)=O_{P}(1),

where the stochastic boundedness of
\Phi^{\prime}_{n},\Psi^{\prime}_{n}
follows from (
27
) and (
13
).
Now, by definition (
27
),
\widetilde{C}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\sqrt{h}\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)+\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds+a^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon)
. Also, with the notation
\eta_{2}^{\prime}(\zeta)=\zeta^{-Y}-1
, the term
{a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=(1-\widetilde{\eta}_{2})a_{2}(\varepsilon)}=:\kappa_{2}^{\prime}h\varepsilon^{-Y}
satisfies

\begin{gathered}a_{2}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon)-a^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon)=\eta_{2}^{\prime}(\zeta_{2})a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon),\\
a^{\prime}_{2}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\varepsilon)-2a^{\prime}_{2}(\zeta_{2}\varepsilon))+a^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon)=(\eta_{2}^{\prime})^{2}(\zeta_{2})a^{\prime}_{2}(h,\varepsilon).\end{gathered}

Therefore, we may express

\displaystyle\widehat{a}_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\eta_{2}^{\prime}a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}u_{n}\Phi^{\prime}_{n})^{2}}{(\eta_{2}^{\prime})^{2}a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}u_{n}\Psi_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\frac{\sqrt{h}a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)u_{n}(2\Phi^{\prime}_{n}-\Psi^{\prime}_{n})+hu_{n}^{2}(\Phi_{n}^{\prime})^{2}}{(\eta_{2}^{\prime})^{2}a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}u_{n}\Psi_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}\times\frac{O_{P}(u_{n})+O_{P}(h^{-3/2}\varepsilon^{4})}{(\eta_{2}^{\prime})^{2}\kappa_{2}^{\prime}+o_{P}(1)}

\displaystyle=a_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{h}\times O_{P}(u_{n}),

where, in the last equality we used that
\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{2/(4+Y)}
to conclude that

\displaystyle(a_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}\sqrt{h}u_{n}

\displaystyle=O_{P}(h^{-1}\varepsilon^{Y}\varepsilon^{\frac{4-Y}{2}})=O_{P}(h^{-1}\varepsilon^{\frac{4+Y}{2}})=o_{P}(1),

\displaystyle(a_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}h^{1/2}u_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=h^{-3/2}\varepsilon^{4}\ll u_{n}=h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{4-Y}{2}}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime\prime}({\varepsilon,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1}})-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)

\displaystyle=h^{-1/2}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{2}_{s}ds-\widehat{a}^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=h^{-1/2}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\zeta_{1})-\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds-a^{\prime}_{2}(\varepsilon)\right)+O(u_{n})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)+O_{P}(u_{n})\overset{st}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,2\int_{0}^{1}\sigma^{4}_{s}ds\right),

where the third and fourth limit follow from (
27
) and (
28
), respectively.
∎

Appendix B
Asymptotic expansions for truncated moments

In this section, we provide high-order asymptotic expansions for the truncated moments of the Itô semimartingale
X
.
As in
A
, we denote
C
or
K
generic constants that may be different from line to line.

To simplify some proofs, we now lay out some additional notation related to the process
J
.
Let
N
be the Poisson jump measure of
J
and let
\bar{N}
be its compensated measure. Observe that due to condition (ii) in Assumption
1
, there exists
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
q(x)>0
for all
|x|\leq{}\delta_{0}
. Next, let
\breve{J}
be a pure-jump Lévy process independent of
J
with triplet
(0,0,\breve{\nu})
, where
\breve{\nu}(dx)=e^{-|x|^{p}}(C_{+}{\bf 1}_{(0,\infty)}(x)+C_{-}{\bf 1}_{(-\infty,0)}(x)){\bf 1}_{|x|>\delta_{0}}\,|x|^{-1-Y}dx
, for a fixed
p<1\wedge Y
, and define the Lévy process

J^{\infty}_{t}=\Big(\bar{b}+\int_{\delta_{0}<|x|\leq 1}x\nu(dx)\Big)t+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|x|\leq{}\delta_{0}}x\bar{N}(ds,dx)+\breve{J}_{t}.

(29)

In other words,
J^{\infty}
has Lévy measure
\nu(dx){\bf 1}_{\{|x|\leq\delta_{0}\}}+\breve{\nu}(dx){\bf 1}_{\{|x|>\delta_{0}\}}
, and, in particular,
J^{\infty}_{t}
satisfies all the conditions of assumptions of
C
,
and, thus, we can apply the asymptotics of the truncated moments established therein.
Next, we write

J^{0}_{t}:=J_{t}-J^{\infty}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|x|>{}\delta_{0}}x{N}(ds,dx)-\breve{J}_{t},

(30)

and observe
J^{0}
has finite jump activity.

As an intermediate step, we first establish estimates for the truncated moments of the process

\displaystyle X_{t}^{\prime}:=\int_{0}^{t}b_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}dW_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{s}dJ^{\infty}_{s}.

(31)

In a subsequent step, we show the same estimates also hold for
X
up to asymptotically negligible terms (Lemma
5
, below).
Note that

X_{t}-X^{\prime}_{t}=X_{t}^{j,0}+\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{s}dJ_{s}^{0}.

In other words, the process
X^{\prime}
includes the continuous component of
X
and the infinite variation component
\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{s}dJ^{\infty}_{s}
, and
X-X^{\prime}
contains only finite variation terms.

Lemma 2
.

Let

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)=\frac{\bar{C}|\chi_{t_{i-1}}|^{Y}}{2-Y}\varepsilon^{2-Y}-\bar{C}\frac{(Y+1)(Y+2)}{2Y}\sigma_{t_{i-1}}^{2}|\chi_{t_{i-1}}|^{Y}\ h\varepsilon^{-Y}.

(32)

Suppose that
Y\in(0,1)\cup(1,\frac{8}{5})
and
h_{n}^{\frac{3}{2(2+Y)}\wedge\frac{1}{2}}\ll\varepsilon_{n}\ll h_{n}^{\frac{1}{4-s}}
, for some
s\in(0,4)
.
Then, for any
i=1,\dots,n
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i-1}\left[(\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime})^{2}{\bf 1}_{\{|\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}|\leq{}\varepsilon\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t_{i-1}}^{2}h+\widehat{A}_{i}(\varepsilon,h)h+o_{P}(h^{3/2}).

(33)

Proof.

We use the following notation

\displaystyle x_{i}:=b_{t_{i-1}}h+\sigma_{t_{i-1}}\Delta_{i}^{n}W+\chi_{t_{i-1}}\Delta_{i}^{n}J^{\infty}=:x_{i,1}+x_{i,2}+x_{i,3},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i}:=\Delta_{i}^{n}X^{\prime}-x_{i},\quad\mathcal{E}_{i,1}=\int_{t_{i-1}}^{t_{i}}b_{s}ds-{b}_{t_{i-1}}h,

(34)
